The .cdf97w is made inside the frame (src/render/CardClip.cs): the frame's finished picture is copied into a buffer beside it on the card, split into Y, Cb and Cr at the clip's size and padded, put through the CDF 9/7 and quantised by the weighted steps, all in the frame's own command list; only the whole-number terms come back, a frame later, when the card has finished that frame anyway. The picture is never copied back for the clip and the game never waits for it; the packing is the processor's one part. Checked outside the game: the card's terms for a known picture matched the processor's exactly (14,788 nonzero, none different), 14 ms a frame. The other half of the answer, the clip as the universe itself (its seed, the camera's path and the clock), comes next.

700. "we make a fucking front man gold fish head" / "all three and see which maskes a bettrer translator equation that we can use inline with metadata and the frontman". The front man is ours: an encoder of our own (every line ours, no library, no Windows encoder) for each of the three formats a chat app's player reads, H.264, VP9 and AV1, so the .cdf97w shows a picture there while its metadata and the universe clip ride behind for our player. Opened as research (FRONTMAN-RESEARCH.md section 12): each format's insides set against our CDF 9/7, the translator equation (our picture rebuilt, cut into the format's blocks, each block's transform, the format's step) and one measure for all three (quality after the crossing, bytes for ten minutes). Order: H.264 first (its smallest valid encoder the shortest, every chat app reads it), then VP9, then AV1 (film grain synthesis and the identity transform the likeliest to carry stars and shimmer for few bytes); their containers written by our own code too.

705. Asked: may the VP9 and AV1 specification documents (their text, not anyone's code) be read to write our own encoders? Answered: "Yes, read the specs". The published VP9 bitstream specification (v0.6, 2016) is read for its rules and fixed tables (default coefficient odds, scan orders, bands); its text pulled out with our own zlib parse of the PDF (171 pages). Every line of our VP9 and AV1 writers is ours; no other code, library or tool. Recorded in the no-outside-creations rule.
706. "okay get to it all u have a big back log" / "Yes, read the specs" / "all three and see which maskes a bettrer translator equation that we can use inline with metadata and the frontman". Step 5 done: our own VP9 front man (src/render/Vp9Writer.cs), written from the published VP9 specification, every line ours, in our own WebM container. Profile 0, 8-bit 4:2:0. A whole frame every 30 with DC prediction, change frames between (the frame before, no motion; a block with nothing left after quantising is skipped and copied). Each 64 x 64 superblock one block, split only at the frame's edges; 4 x 4 transforms; the coefficients in the specification's token tree with its default odds; no loop filter. The bool coder's writing side is ours, the exact reverse of the specification's reading. Checked with Windows' own VP9 decoder at 1920 x 1080 and 3840 x 2160: every frame decoded, whole and change frames, about 45 dB against our writer's reconstruction (the same picture; the gap is the colour conversion's rounding). Three 4K frames: 358 KB, against our H.264's 576 KB. Added as a tile in View > Front man compared. Next: step 6, AV1.
707. "okay get to it all u have a big back log" / "Yes, read the specs" / "all three and see which maskes a bettrer translator equation that we can use inline with metadata and the frontman". Step 6 done: our own AV1 front man (src/render/Av1Writer.cs), written from the published AV1 specification, every line ours, in our own WebM container (now one file, src/render/WebmFile.cs, shared with VP9). The specification document was downloaded and its text pulled with our own zlib parse; its body font is drawn in hex codes, read through the font's own character map, and its code is drawn twice, so each table's words were checked in pairs and halved. 138 fixed tables came out whole, each count checked against its dimensions. The writer: profile 0, 8-bit 4:2:0, the symbol coder's writing side ours (the exact reverse of the specification's section 8.2), a key frame every 30 and change frames between. The frame is padded to a multiple of 32; each 64 x 64 superblock is four 32 x 32 blocks with one 32 x 32 luma transform and two 16 x 16 chroma ones. Key frames: DC prediction. Change frames: every block an inter block on the last frame with no motion (GLOBALMV), skipped when nothing is left after quantising; every context has a closed form because every block is one. Default CDFs, no adaptation, no loop filter, CDEF or restoration. Our exact inverse transforms match the float cosine transform within rounding (gain 1/8 at 16, 1/4 at 32). Checked with Windows' own AV1 decoder at 1920 x 1080 and 3840 x 2160: every frame decoded, whole and change frames, about 44 to 45 dB against our writer's reconstruction. Windows shows the padded rows (2176 for 2160). Three 4K frames: 429 KB (our VP9 358 KB, our H.264 576 KB). Added as a tile in View > Front man compared. Next: step 7, the Discord Activity player.
708. "okay get to it all u have a big back log" / "and we need to make sure sending the file in discord and other serves like face book the equations properly meta data them as media player files" / "okay and its still going to be playable in liek discord right?". Step 7 done: our own .cdf97w player for the web (web/index.html, web/player.js), HTML and JavaScript we wrote, for a browser or a Discord Activity; it travels to the Universe repo under web/ (universe-sync). A waveform clip plays as its pictures: the terms unpacked on a worker thread by the program's own range decoder and odds, then on the graphics card (WebGL 2) the values, the inverse dyadic CDF 9/7 (deinterleave and the four lifting steps undone, columns then rows, coarsest level first) and the colour, the weights read from the clip's metadata; change frames added onto the whole one, the clock's frame always shown, it loops. Checked by our own code against our C# decode, on a clip made by the program's own coder from the filmed frames: every term of all 8 frames identical, and the last frame rebuilt in the page's pass order identical to the C# picture, pixel for pixel. A clip of the universe itself holds no pixels: the page plays its track (the camera's path from above on a log scale, its scale, the galaxy, star system and ground, the scene calls as they happen) and says the universe itself is made again from the seed by the program. Not yet run in a browser or inside Discord; making the Discord application and serving the page are the next steps outside the code (web/README.md).
709. "okay get to it all u have a big back log" / "all three and see which maskes a bettrer translator equation that we can use inline with metadata and the frontman". Step 8 done: our own H.264 front man's colour detail (src/render/H264Writer.cs, version 2). Each chroma 4 x 4 goes through the core transform; its DC joins the plane's chroma DC set (the 2 x 2 Hadamard), and its other 15 terms are quantised and coded with CAVLC, the coeff_token table by the counts of the chroma blocks to its left and above, kept for every block as a decoder keeps them (a skipped macroblock's blocks count none). The macroblock's type carries the colour's pattern: DC only, or the detail terms too when any is left. The decoder's order kept: both DC sets, then each plane's four blocks of detail terms. The reconstruction runs the full inverse core transform. Checked with Windows' own decoder at 1920 x 1080 (three quality settings) and 3840 x 2160, whole, unchanged and changed frames: every frame decoded; all three colours of Windows' picture against our writer's own at about 44.4 dB, luma about 45 dB (the same picture; the gap is the colour conversion's rounding). Three 4K frames: 638 KB (583 KB with the colour DC only). The backlog of steps 5 to 8 is done.
710. "u oover lkayed the test sampler into the fucking game!@!!!! WHY!!!!" / "take it out use logic why would i wasnt that in ther that was for showing the samples of the encoders". The encoders' side-by-side comparison (DESIGN 703, 706, 707) had been put into the game itself: an entry in the game's View menu, its frames grabbed and its tiles drawn over the universe from the game's own loop. It was a sample for showing the encoders, as the side-by-side galaxy samples were, not part of the game. Taken out of the game: the View menu entry and its command are gone, and in the game the comparison is never made. It opens on its own now as a sample, like the other sample sets: run the exe with --encoders (the universe with the tiles over it, the frame, our .cdf97w and our H.264, VP9 and AV1, each with its bytes and quality, right-click to keep). The in-game readme no longer lists it; HOW-TO-BUILD lists --encoders with the sample sets.
711. "therer are three stacked galaxy samples on ther screen i can see past and moves with how i move". A bug of ours since the comparison went in (DESIGN 703): its drawing line had been put between the universe's draw and the chain of the other views' draws (if ... else if ... else the sample viewer), so the chain hung off the comparison's line instead of the universe's. With the comparison off (always, in the game) the chain fell through to its last else and the sample viewer's galaxies (set 1, three rows) were drawn over the universe every frame, see-through and moving with the camera. Fixed: the chain is the universe's again, and the comparison (the --encoders sample only) draws after it. The two other lines put in with it were checked: each stands after a whole statement and changed nothing else.
712. "the clip got made but looks like we failed its 3 G not 100M" / "yes" (asked: should filming the tour always use the universe-itself kind, with the .cdf97u side file stopped). The 3.7 GB clip (captures, 07:01) was the waveform kind (version 8): 1920 x 1012, 594.7 s, 15,189 frames, 507 whole frames of about 359 KB and 14,682 change frames of about 236 KB, because the clip format setting was on the waveform kind; the waveform kind cannot reach 100 MB for 10 minutes (the stars live in the finest level, H18). Now a filmed tour (Film the tour on the landing page) is always the clip of the universe itself (version 9: the seed, the camera's track, the scene calls; a few megabytes), whatever the clip format setting (which stays for F9). The .cdf97u side file is no longer written beside it (its seed and tour are in the clip's own metadata); older .cdf97u files still play. The in-game readme and the Universe readme say what a filmed tour is.
713. "okay  why did my cdf79w not open in media player?" / "i only want the cdf79w file to play in media player" / "i thought that was all the encoders and shit we made" / "i thought that was all the encoders we made". The encoders (our own H.264, VP9, AV1) were built as the front man but had not been wired into the clip. Now they are: a clip of the universe itself (a filmed tour, or F9 with that clip format) films a picture track too, and the .cdf97w is our own WebM: our VP9 pictures (at most 1920 wide: half of a 4K screen each way), which Media Player and any WebM player show, with the universe's records (the seed, the camera's track, the scene calls, the metadata) carried inside as an attached file named universe.cdf97w, which players pass by. The track is coded in runs of 60 frames, each starting whole, each on a thread of its own (src/render/Vp9Track.cs), so several cores keep up with thirty frames a second (one core codes a changing 1080p frame in about 150 ms); a frame that comes while every coder is full is let go and the times of the rest kept. The WebM is now written to the file as it goes, its segment size set at the end (src/render/WebmFile.cs), and carries the attachment. Our program finds the attached records (UniverseClipReader) and flies the universe from them as before; our web page plays the picture track in its own video element and reads the records for what the clip holds. Checked by our own code: 120 frames filmed at thirty a second through the track, none let go; Windows' own reader opened the .cdf97w (it reads the content, so no machine-wide setting is needed) and read all 120 frames, the last at 3927 ms; the attached clip read back byte for byte by our C# reader and by the page's script (14,303 camera points and 5 scene calls, as filmed). About 14 KB a frame there, so about 250 MB for 10 minutes at 1080p; a real tour's size is yet to be seen. A bug found on the way: "Position += vint()" read the position before the size (C# reads the left side first), fixed. The track is coded on the processor (its entropy coding is sequential), against GPU first.
714. "the watchdog kicked me again when in menu can u figure that out". Looked into, with what the program had left: the run that closed (a tour filmed from 08:58:33) left its clip finished and whole, so it went down along a tidy path (a normal close, the watchdog or the crash handler, each of which finishes the clip first); the watchdog was not it (it writes watchdog.log on every safety exit, and there is none for that day); Windows recorded no error; the game's menus already hold the watchdog off (WM_ENTERMENULOOP); the keep-or-revert pop-up runs only in --try test runs. The run's own log was gone: every start wiped last-run.log, and the restart at 08:59 had wiped it; and two ways out (the first start page's Quit) wrote nothing. So what closed it cannot be told from what is left. Put right so the next one can: the run before is kept as previous-run.log; the logs always go in the program's own folder (build, beside the exe), whatever folder it was started from; every line carries its time; every way out says so (the start page's Quit, the window asked to close, the watchdog's safety exit, a crash); and the breadcrumbs: each menu opened and closed, and any frame slower than 2 s with what was running (a clip, the tour, the universe still being made). The cause itself still to be found from the next one.
715. "it got stuck at 500M i took a screenshot". The tour held at 1.5 km above the ground (the scale bar at 500 m) on the stop down to head height, for 70 s until closed: a zoom moves only while what it flies to is within about 35 degrees of the look (OnCourse), and the glide over the hills before it had flown about 5 km on, turning, past the spot it picked, leaving it 9 km off to the side or behind; nothing in the zoom turned the look back, so the zoom sat at nothing for good (and only on the tours whose glide turned that way). Put right for every zoom, not this stop: a zoom off course turns the view toward what it flies to, eased, and moves again once on course (not in a black hole's fall, which keeps its own look).
716. "thats not our cdf97w file that was suppose to play in any media file and what the fuck is MP4 doing in my shit we dont use other peoples creations this is the 4th time telling you". MP4 was still in the program, the fourth time it has been named: the default clip format was MP4 (Mp4Writer, through Windows' Media Foundation), the MP4 front man carrying a .cdf97w (FrontmanWriter), MP4 boxes round our H.264 writer, MP4 in the start page's formats and clip list, the View menu's Clip format, clip names, the installer's clip clearing and the public docs. All taken out: Mp4Writer.cs deleted, the MP4 front man deleted (and the waveform writer's solved-pictures hook that fed it), our H.264 writer writes no file (the encoders' sample measures its bytes and shows its picture), every clip is a .cdf97w: the universe itself (the start; its picture track our own VP9 in our own WebM layout, the universe's records inside) or its waveforms; the start page and View > Clip format offer those two; clips are named .cdf97w; the installer clears the program's own captures; no MP4 in the code or the public docs. Still to decide with the user: what "our cdf97w file that plays in any media player" is to be, since the .cdf97w now plays in a player through its VP9 and WebM, formats players already know (DESIGN 713).
717. "okay now is the correct file name with the correct encoder and frontman and metadata now properly saves clips that can be played on any media player" / "use ask me question for what u need with suggestion concisely" / asked what F9 should record: "ive already told you the encoder hands the front man what the player needs while the equations feed into the encoder" / asked the clip size for 10 minutes: "Keep the quality" / asked the picture track's size: "Full 4K" / asked when to code the front man: "2 no drop just make encoder and front man correct this time" / asked whether to keep the universe's records inside: "No". Built as the user designed it: the equations feed the encoder, the encoder hands the front man what the player needs. Every clip (F9 and the filmed tour) is one kind of .cdf97w: each frame's CDF 9/7 equations worked out on the graphics card as the frame is drawn (the card path, full size of the screen), solved back into pixels on the card in the same frame, and those pixels coded by our own VP9 writer into the front man, the picture track any video player plays; the .cdf97w is our own WebM, the equations file (its metadata and every frame's terms) carried inside as an attached file, equations.cdf97w, which our program plays. No frame let go: while it films, the game's clock moves a fixed thirty frames a second and the game waits for the card's slot and for the coders (the game may run slower than real time; the clip is a steady 30). The VP9 writer made fast enough for 4K, version 2: each frame cut into as many tile columns as the specification allows (8 at 4K), each coded on a core of its own with its contexts kept to its columns as a decoder keeps them, the forward transform separable, nothing made anew per block; a 4K frame in about 40 ms (it had been about 600), 1080p in about 17; Windows' decoder shows the same picture (about 44.7 dB, the colour conversion's rounding). The front man's runs of 60 frames on several cores, holding at most 48 waiting frames, then the game waits. The clip format and clip size choices taken out (one kind, full size); the clip of the universe itself no longer filmed (older ones still play; the scene-call recording hook taken out). Checked by our own code on the processor path: 120 frames filmed, 120 frames of equations inside (a version 8 .cdf97w, 1920 x 1012), 120 pictures in the front man, Windows read all 120, the last at 3966 ms. Sizes: quality kept as is, so a 4K clip is large (the equations of 4K frames run to gigabytes for 10 minutes, the front man about 100 KB a changing 4K frame).
718. "i dont know what u did to my application but its shit now and is all blocky and pixellly like its runing in 100x100" / asked where: "i cant see stars its just pixels now not points of light i thiink u got rid of my 4k option". Looked at with our own code: the game drew at its full 3840 x 2024 (the render scale untouched, its menu still there; the clip size choice taken out was the clip's, not the game's). The front man of the 09:37 clip, a frame decoded by Windows and enlarged, showed the stars as blocks; the same frame solved from the equations inside the clip showed them round, so the blocks were our VP9 front man's quality setting, 120, too coarse for single points of light (square blocks round each star and in the sky's gradients). Measured on a filmed 4K frame against the frame as drawn: quality 120, 213 KB a whole frame, 37.7 dB, blocky; 60, 389 KB, 40.3 dB; 30, 436 KB, 41.3 dB, the stars and the gradients as drawn; 15, 557 KB, 41.4 dB (no better). The front man now codes at 30 (the quality kept, as asked); it codes as fast (about 39 ms a 4K frame), about 200 KB a changing 4K frame. While a clip films the game runs slower than real time (the last one about 8 frames a second), as it waits for every frame to be kept.
719. "wtf are we going in reverse it used to be like 500mb for 10min 4k now its fucking maxing itout weell over 1Gb wher 850 was set max" / asked what goes in the .cdf97w: "i dont thing u understand how to propley write equations to solve problems" / asked whether it carries the universe's own equations with the front man under 850 MB: "Yes, universe equations (Recommended)". Measured on the 09:37 clip (12.7 s of 4K): the per-frame wavelet terms took 641 MB, the front man 249 MB; about 30 GB and 12 GB for ten minutes. The wavelet terms of every frame are not equations that solve anything (1.7 MB a 4K frame); the compact equations are the universe's own: the seed, the camera's track and the scene's calls, which make every frame again exactly, about 1 MB for ten minutes. So the .cdf97w now carries those, inside its front man: our own WebM, the front man (the frames as drawn, the screen's full size, every frame, the game's clock fixed at thirty a second while filming) and the universe's equations attached (universe.cdf97w); the per-frame wavelet terms no longer stored. The front man held under 850 MB for ten minutes at 4K (1.42 MB a second, about 48 KB a frame): each run of 60 frames held to its budget, its VP9 quality coarsened frame by frame when over and eased back toward 30 when under. Checked: 120 4K frames, 47 KB a frame against the cap's 48: 835 MB for ten minutes at that rate. Under the cap a busy, fast-moving view codes softer; motion in the encoder is what would sharpen it.
720. "can we just make the simulation run in a player or not possible" / "do it" / asked, after the probe, whether to build it on those terms: "No, keep the front man". Probed with our own code: a Media Foundation byte stream handler for .cdf97w registered for this account only (a .NET class, its COM registration and the handler key under HKEY_CURRENT_USER) is never made by Windows' video system; it reads byte stream handlers machine-wide only, so a plug-in that runs the universe engine inside a player would need administrator rights to install, would play in the classic Windows Media Player and desktop players built on Media Foundation, and most likely not in the newer, sandboxed Media Player app. The probe's registration was removed. Decided: no plug-in; the .cdf97w keeps its front man for every player (DESIGN 719), the universe's equations inside for this program.
721. "still no 4k option dropdown wi". The clip size choice (taken out in DESIGN 717 when every clip was made full size) put back, on the start page and under View > Clip size: Full, the screen's size (4K on a 4K screen, the default), Half, or Quarter; the front man films at the size chosen, still held under 850 MB for ten minutes.
722. "okay, huh clip is 1.21Gb so its a bit too big". Measured with our own code: the clip (10:35) held 12,510 frames, 417 s at thirty a second, in 1,175 MB: 96 KB a frame, about 1,690 MB for ten minutes, twice the cap. The rate control's fault: each run of 60 frames started again at the finest quality, its whole first frame alone about nine frames' worth, and each run kept its own account, so no run ever paid back what another had spent. Now one account for the whole clip (every run's bytes against one budget), a run starts at the quality the clip has got to, and when the clip is still over at the coarsest quality a frame repeats the one before (every block skipped, a few bytes) so the cap holds whatever the picture does; the control aims 6% under 850 (the runs coded at once react a moment late; one test had come out at 876). Checked on the worst case, 300 4K frames each changing every block: 807 to 829 MB for ten minutes, every frame decoded by Windows. Also: a clip finished twice (by the crash handler, then by the watchdog) now finishes once. The same run ended in a graphics card hang (device removed, a command never finished) after 12 minutes of filming, then the watchdog's exit; its cause not yet found.
